Market analysis

OIL FUTURES: Crude edges higher as market weighs Middle East escalation risks
Hormuz ship transits rise despite attacks
Analysts see 45% chance conflict escalates
Crude oil futures edged higher Nov. 6, but finished the week sharply lower as markets weighed outlooks for potential Middle East escalation following the US midterm elections.
NYMEX December WTI ended the Nov. 6 session up 31 cents/ barrel at $90.83/b, but finished the week down nearly 5.5% from the date of the US midterm elections on Nov. 3. ICE January Brent climbed 3 cents/b but ended the week still down 4.5% from Nov. 3.
"We view this as the reversal of substantial risk premium that had accrued across September and October despite resilient Middle East flows and rapidly normalizing global oil balances," said Walt Chancellor, an energy strategist at Macquarie Group, in a Nov. 6 note. "This has now given way to a renewed diplomatic push, less hawkish rhetoric from Trump, and apparent Republican introspection about the war in the aftermath of the midterms."
The presumed incoming Democrat leaders of the US Senate and House of Representatives, minority leaders Chuck Schumer and Hakeem Jeffries, have called on the Trump administration to end the Middle East war.
"With the Democrats taking control of both chambers of Congress in January, they will have greater scope to pressure the administration through investigations, legislation, and restrictions on war funding. However, Trump still retains considerable freedom of action in foreign policy, and there is no agreement

International Crude Asian refiners eye North African crude to diversify despite softer Dubai structure Platts Dubai price structure falls to prewar level Pakistan bags El Sharara crude at Platts Dated Brent
plus $20/b Thailand triples, South Korea doubles North African imports
The recent weakening of the Dubai crude market structure may signal some improvement in sour-crude flows through the Strait of Hormuz, but Asian refiners plan to continue diversifying their feedstock by increasing US crude purchases and actively seeking spot cargoes from North Africa, according to government ministries and industry sources over Oct. 26-Nov. 5.
It is far too early to assume that tanker flows through the Strait of Hormuz will fully normalize based solely on the US midterm election results, feedstock managers at refineries in Negishi, Karachi, Chonburi and Seosan, as well as Pakistani and South Korean government ministries, said over Oct. 26-Nov. 5.
Platts assessed the spread between Cash Dubai and samemonth Dubai futures at 30 cents/b on Nov. 2, the lowest level since minus 30 cents/b on July 17 and below the prewar February monthly average of 92 cents/b. Platts last assessed the spread at $1.46/b on Nov. 5. The spread -- widely known as the Dubai M1-M3 market structure -- is a key component of the monthly official selling price calculations used by major Middle Eastern producers.
The significant pullback in the Dubai market structure in recent trading sessions may reflect improved sour-crude flows through the Strait of Hormuz. However, given the fluid geopolitical landscape, it remains important to continue actively seeking and procuring

non-Middle Eastern crude, the Japanese, Thai, South Korean and Pakistani refinery feedstock managers told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, during market discussions over Oct. 26-Nov. 4.
"Supply-chain instability, including disruptions to crude oil and energy flows, is a structural risk that the economy must continue to manage," South Korean Deputy Prime Minister Lee Hyung-il was quoted as saying in the fourth-quarter South Korean government policy briefing report Nov. 3. "Proactive efforts must be made to strengthen and broaden supply chain options."
Oil shipping through the Strait of Hormuz is unlikely to fully recover even if peace is achieved following the US midterm elections, President and CEO Takaya Soga of Japan's Nippon Yusen Kaisha said Nov. 5.
Asia's major US crude buyers -- South Korea, Japan and Thailand -- as well as Pakistan, would continue purchasing WTI Midland crude to reduce their reliance on Middle Eastern suppliers, while regularly monitoring the availability of spot cargoes from alternative suppliers, including Libya and Algeria, as part of their diversification efforts, according to the feedstock managers and a statement from Pakistan's Petroleum Ministry.
North African crude Pakistan Refinery Ltd. has sought two to three cargoes of
Libya's El Sharara crude, each carrying about 70,000 metric tons, at a premium of $20/b to Platts Dated Brent on a delivered basis, a refinery official with direct knowledge of the deal told Platts. The Ministry of Petroleum also indicated the deal in its statement.
Compared with WTI Midland -- Asia's most popular non-Middle Eastern crude -- light sweet Libyan crude is somewhat more competitive, even after accounting for this year's high freight rates, sweet crude traders based in Singapore said.

as it is difficult to assess when Qatari ultra light crude supply and trade flows will fully return to normal, a company feedstock inventory manager told Platts Nov. 4.
South Korea -- Asia's third-biggest crude buyer -- imported 24,000 b/d of Libyan crude, mostly Mellitah condensate, in the first nine months, compared with zero shipments in 2025, the latest data from state-run Korea National Oil Corp. issued Oct. 30 showed.
In addition, crude imports from another North African market, Algeria, totaled 63,000 b/d over January-September, almost double the 39,000 b/d purchased a year earlier, KNOC data showed.
In Southeast Asia, light sweet Libyan grades have been an important non-Middle Eastern alternative for Thailand, the feedstock manager at a Chonburi-based refinery told Platts.

Platts assessed El Sharara's official selling price differential against Dated Brent at a premium of $1/b for November, down from a premium of $4.50/b in October.
Petroleum Minister Ali Pervaiz held bilateral talks with Libyan Oil and Gas Minister Khalifa Rajab Abdulsadek in Riyadh in early October and encouraged Pakistani refineries to explore imports of Libyan crude, the Ministry of Petroleum said in the statement.
In North Asia, South Korean refining and petrochemical company Hanwha TotalEnergies started picking up Libyan Mellitah condensate for the first time in more than five years,

Thailand imported 43,300 b/d of crude from Libya in the first nine months, almost triple the 16,800 b/d purchased during the same period a year earlier, Thai customs data showed.
-- Gawoon Vahn, Takeo Kumagai, Haris Zamir
Med sour crude differentials fall as Iraqi flows reach all-time high
Basrah Medium falls below Dated Brent: traders Discharges of Kirkuk more than double on month: Kpler Outlook bearish on ample supply, North Sea softening
The value of Iraq's Basrah Medium crude in the Mediterranean fell over $3/barrel week over week to a $1.50/b discount to

Platts Oilgram Price Report

November 6, 2026

December average Dated Brent Nov. 5, traders indicated to Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, as October flows of the grade to the Med, along with those for Kirkuk blend, reached the highest level on record, tracking data showed.
In October, the Mediterranean imported over 20 million barrels of the two Iraqi crudes, surpassing the previous record of 19 million barrels in October 2023, Kpler shipping data showed Nov. 6.
The increase was driven by discharges of Kirkuk which rose to 8.9 million barrels in October, up from 3.8 million barrels in September, tracking data showed.
Kirkuk, a blend of different Iraqi crude oils, is transported via pipeline to the Turkish port of Ceyhan in the Mediterranean, where it's loaded onto ships. Availability of Kirkuk improved recently as more volume was piped from Iraq to the Mediterranean, Platts reported earlier.
The barrels were last heard at around flat to Dated Brent for December loading, maintaining a quality spread of around $1.50/b above Basrah Medium.
"The market is pretty well-supplied at the moment," one Mediterranean trader said. "And I expect that to be reflected in lower December OSPs [for Kirkuk] once they're out."
Flows of Basrah Medium also rose, albeit less sharply, and are close to levels last seen in January 2026. In October, the Med received 11.2 million barrels of the grade, just below 11.8 million barrels seen in January, Kpler data showed.
Although most participants value Kirkuk around $1-$2/b above Basrah Medium, the spread can narrow occasionally to reflect

refiners' preference for Basrah. "Mediterranean refiners see Basrah as having a reliable
supply," a second Mediterranean trader said. "They just 'know it' and are accustomed to processing it."
But with the bitumen season just over, the sour complex is seasonally weaker, adding to downward pressures on differentials. On top of that, the European complex has come off in recent days with the Dated Brent differential falling into negative territory.
"I am bearish on sweets because of what's happening with Dated Brent, but in this environment, I am even more bearish on sour grades," a third trader said.
-- Jan Kedzior
No plans to resume Kazakh Druzhba oil flows to Germany until end-2026: ministry
Says no exports planned for November, December
Russia suspended transit citing technical issues
Schwedt refinery loses about 20% of daily supply
There are no plans to resume Kazakh crude flows through the Druzhba pipeline to Germany's PCK Schwedt refinery until the end of 2026, according to Kazakhstan's energy ministry Nov. 6.
"No exports of Kazakhstani oil to Germany are planned for November and December 2026," the ministry told InterfaxKazakhstan news agency.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

November 6, 2026

transit flows is pending, the ministry said. The Kazakh energy ministry said Nov. 5 that it has sent
an official letter to Russia's energy ministry requesting the resumption of Kazakh oil transportation via the Druzhba pipeline system and shipment through the Baltic port of Ust-Luga.
Russia suspended the Druzhba transit route for Kazakh oil at the beginning of May. Moscow cited "technical capabilities" as the reason for the halt, without providing further details.
The loss of Kazakh Druzhba flows has affected about 20% of the 230,000 barrels/day Schwedt refinery, the German energy ministry said. Despite the disruptions, the refinery's utilization remained stable at about 85%-90%, with alternative supply organized.
"Should the existing technical constraints be resolved and the necessary transportation conditions be met, the possibility of resuming shipments of Kazakhstani oil along this route will be considered," the Kazakh ministry said Nov. 6.
Both Russian and Kazakh ministries did not respond to Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, requests for comment Nov. 6.
The last time crude of Kazakh origin was loaded at the UstLuga port on the Baltic Sea was in August, S&P Global Energy Commodities at Sea data showed. Russia loaded only about 11 million barrels in Ust-Luga in October, the lowest for any month but one since February 2021.
Before the halt, Ust-Luga Kazakh crude loadings averaged 1.75 million barrels/month in January-August.
The Ust-Luga port came under Ukraine's attack the last time Sept. 1, with fire and damage reported at the terminal.
Kazakhstan's state energy company KazMunayGas renewed its deal with Rosneft Deutschland in October 2025 to deliver up to 130,000 metric tons/month of Kazakh crude to its Schwedt refinery, up from a previous 100,000 mt/month, effective through 2026.
Kazakhstan supplied 730,000 metric tons of oil through the Transneft-operated pipeline system for onward delivery to Germany in the first quarter of 2026, an increase of 353,000 mt compared to the same period in 2025, according to Kazakhstan's national oil transporter, KazTransOil, data.
-- Alexandra Vladimirova
Russian crude exports fall on G7 tankers as Greek operators flee for better earnings
Greek tankers exit Russian routes for profits, security
G7 tankers' market share in Russia hits 7-month low
Non-G7 fleet maintains shipments amid relief talk
Russian crude exports on G7 tankers fell sharply in October as Greek operators were enticed by strong earnings elsewhere and steered away from Black Sea conflicts, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea and Maritime Intelligence Risk Suite data.
The data shows tankers flagged, owned or operated by companies based in G7 countries and their allies, or insured by Western protection and indemnity clubs, loaded roughly 906,000

barrels per day of Russian crude in October, or 24.2% of total exports.
The share was the lowest in seven months. In September, G7 tankers lifted 1.2 million b/d, or 29.2% of total exports.
Shipments by tanker operators in Greece, the top shipowning nation within G7/EU, more than halved to 8.3 million barrels in October from 17.3 million barrels in September, according to CAS/ MIRS. October's loadings were the lowest since December 2024.
The development came as tanker rates were around their all-time highs in mainstream markets, boosted by larger ship requirements from offshore transfers east of the Strait of Hormuz and high risk premiums during the ongoing US-Iran war.
October's average Suezmax time-charter-equivalent earnings for transporting Russian crude from the Baltic to West Coast India was $46,257.92 per day lower than the Global Suezmax Index, compared with a discount of $23,225.69/day in September, according to Platts assessments for non-scrubber, non-eco ships. Platts is part of S&P Global Energy.
In a research note published last month, CAS analysts said four Greek-owned Aframaxes and four Suezmaxes had migrated from Russian to mainstream trades where earnings environments were strong.
Greek tanker firms have also been avoiding operations in the Black Sea, with the CAS/MIRS data showing only one Greekoperated tanker lifting Russian crude in the region last month, compared with 11 in other regions.
The International Maritime Organization said more than 100 ships might have been hit in the Black Sea and Sea of Azov this year as part of the Russia-Ukraine war, potentially killing 38 seafarers.
Platts assessed the additional war risk premium for crude exports from the Black Sea at $5/b on Nov. 6, the highest since the assessment began in October 2022.
Shadow fleet The non-G7 fleet, mainly composed of shadow tankers
amassed by Russia and opaque owners to bypass Western sanctions, lifted 2.8 million b/d of Russian crude in October, according to CAS/MIRS. This compared with 2.9 million b/d in

US Energy Secretary Chris Wright last month said his country planned to allow legitimate businesses to acquire sanctioned tankers for non-sanctioned trades to reduce shipping costs in mainstream trades, though Washington has yet to announce a detailed relief proposal.
A total of 42 tankers have migrated from potentially illicit trades in Russia, Venezuela and Iran to mainstream markets so far this year, according to the CAS note, though none of them have been formally sanctioned by the US.
"Migrated vessels were rapidly absorbed into mainstream Middle East Gulf export chains following their transition into compliant employment," CAS analysts said, adding that a growing proportion of operators have opted to remain in compliant markets once migration occurs.

China's INE crude futures volume drops 46% MOM to four-month low in Oct
Oct traded volume reaches 3.46 million lots
Delivery volume surges tenfold in October
The volume of crude oil futures traded on the Shanghai International Energy Exchange declined 46.15% month over month to 3.46 million lots in October from 6.42 million lots in September, reaching a four-month low, exchange data showed Nov. 6.
The INE crude oil futures contract (SC) is a physical settlement contract priced and traded in yuan, having first been listed in March 2018. One lot is equivalent to 1,000 barrels.
Following suit, the total traded volume for the front-month November SC futures contract was at 606,556 lots in October, decreasing 74.52% from the 2.38 million lots that the then frontmonth October SC futures contract had recorded in September, the exchange data showed.
The INE crude futures contract comprises crudes of mostly medium, sour quality, including grades such as Dubai, Upper Zakum, Murban, Oman, Qatar Marine, Basrah Light, Basrah Medium, Tupi, Buzios, Djeno and Shengli. These are delivered to various storage facilities across coastal China, such as Zhoushan, Dalian and Shandong.
"The overall trading volume was contracting over the month as activity of short-term speculative capital declined," said a source with a futures company.
October also saw fewer trading days in China due to the Golden Week holiday.
Crude throughput at China's state-owned refineries faces further downward pressure in November as high feedstock costs and weakening margins prompt some plants to consider

voluntary run cuts on top of scheduled maintenance, Platts reported earlier.
December cash Dubai assessments averaged $110.73/barrel in October, down from an average of $113.711/b for front-month November cash Dubai in September, while the Dubai cash-futures spread -- a measure of the market structure -- averaged $8.10/b in October, down from $21.60/b in September, data from Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, showed.
Meanwhile, total open interest for the futures contract was 63,195 lots Oct. 30, up 1.02% from 62,556 lots at the end of September.
Contrastingly, the open interest for the front-month November SC futures was at 1,295 lots as of Oct. 30, down 34.60% from the front-month October SC futures at 1980 lots Sept. 30.
The crude oil delivery volume in October was at 1,980 lots, or 1,980,000 barrels, a tenfold rise from that delivered over September.
-- Staff
WAF TRACKING: Crude loadings fall to 3.6 mil b/d in October: CAS
Nigerian loadings in October total 1.525 mil b/d
Bonny Light top loader, averaging 320,000 b/d
Nigerian crude differentials climb on softer freight rates
Average loadings of crude oil from across the West African region fell to some 3.6 million barrels/day in October, down from around 4 million b/d in September, according to the latest S&P Global Commodities at Sea data Nov. 6.
The region's largest producer, Nigeria, maintained its top spot at 1.525 million b/d with Angola in second place at just over 990,000 b/d. The Republic of the Congo was in third place with 256,000 b/d, the majority of which consisted of its main export grade, Djeno.
Djeno was the third-highest export grade in West Africa at 245,000 b/d, beaten by Nigeria's two largest loading crude streams in Bonny Light and Forcados, respectively, at 320,000 b/d and 312,000 b/d.
China was expected to be the largest recipient of the WAF region's October-loading crude at 672,000 b/d, though it remained lower than its pre-Middle East war regular monthly intake. Throughout 2025, China was the destination of some 985,000 b/d of WAF-origin crude oil, CAS data showed.
Volumes headed to Nigeria, mostly for the Dangote refinery, totaled 632,000 b/d in October, with the figure set to represent the highest monthly volume since July when the figure reached 687,000 b/d, and down from 2026's peak of 790,000 b/d in April, according to CAS.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed Nigeria's flagship Bonny Light crude at a 55-cent/b discount to global benchmark Dated Brent on Nov. 5, higher by 25 cents/b day over day, and up from an all-time low of a $2.45/b discount seen as recently as Oct. 22.

SINGAPORE DATA: Crude imports fall 39% week over week to 402,499 mt
Middle East accounts for 67% of oil imports
Saudi crude import volume highest since early June
Australian condensate imported over latest week
Singapore's crude oil imports fell 39.2% week over week to 402,499 metric tons over Oct. 29-Nov. 4, data from Enterprise Singapore showed Nov. 5.
The Middle East accounted for 67% of total crude oil imports, with Saudi Arabia constituting the sole source of imports from the region at 270,177 mt.
This marks the fourth instance of Saudi crude imports into Singapore in the last five reporting weeks, and is also the highest weekly import volume recorded from the Middle East producer since the week ended June 10.
Imports were also recorded from Brazil at 51,398 mt, Canada at 49,470 mt, and South Sudan at 31,454 mt, the data showed.
Australia constituted the sole source of condensate imports for the week at 75,536 mt.
Singapore's crude oil imports averaged 107,699 mt/d in October, up from 99,070 mt/d in September, according to Enterprise Singapore data compiled by Platts. Average imports over Nov. 1-4 stood at 57,500 mt/d.
The city-state's condensate imports averaged 13,158 mt/d over October, up from 9,824 mt/d in September, the data showed. Average condensate imports over Nov. 1-4 stood at 10,791 mt/d.
-- Yong ren Toh
CRUDE MOC: Middle East crude differentials rise for second day
Platts Dubai, Oman premiums up 27 cents/b DOD
8 Dubai partials trade during MOC
Cash differentials for Middle East sour crude markers rose for a second day during the Singapore Platts Market on Close assessment process Nov. 6.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed January cash Dubai and cash Oman both at a premium of $1.73/b to samemonth Dubai futures at market close, up 27 cents/b on the day.
January cash Murban was up 10 cents/b over the same period at a premium of $4.60/b to same-month Dubai futures.
During the MOC, eight January Dubai partials of 25,000 barrels each traded.
The sellers were Mercuria, TotalEnergies and Trafigura, while the buyers were Phillips 66, BP, Vitol, Shenghong and Shell.

No convergences were reached during the MOC. A convergence occurs when 20 partials are traded between two counterparties in the same direction, resulting in a full 500,000-barrel physical cargo being declared from the seller to the buyer.

Americas Crude
October US crude exports edge higher despite soaring freight costs
US crude exports rise to 4.2 million b/d
VLCC freight rates triple in two months
Asian refining margins fall 37% in October
US crude exports edged up to 4.2 million barrels/day in October, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea data, despite soaring VLCC freight rates that weighed on arbitrage economics to Asia.
South Korea was the largest named destination for US WTI crude in October, receiving 14.8 million barrels, although that was slightly below 15.6 million barrels in September.
Shipments to the Netherlands fell to 10.1 million barrels from 11 million barrels, while exports to Canada increased to 8.5 million barrels from 7 million barrels.
Medium and heavy crude exports showed a pronounced shift toward Northeast Asian buyers in October.
Japan received about 4.65 million barrels of medium and heavy crude and South Korea took 3.90 million barrels. Germany received 700,350 barrels, though these figures include re-exports of Canadian-origin crude from the US Gulf Coast.
In September, Japan had received about 1.01 million barrels and South Korea 1.42 million barrels, while the October data showed no medium or heavy shipments to China, India or Spain.
Freight surge squeezes Asia economics The October export increase came despite a steep rise in
tanker costs. USGC-China VLCC freight averaged $244.70/mt, compared with $156.19/mt in September and $79.21/mt in August. Rates therefore more than tripled in the space of two months.
The increase was not confined to China. USGC-Singapore VLCC rates averaged $240.99/mt in October, up from $152.49/ mt in September, while USGC-UKC/Med rates rose to $125.67/mt from $80.82/mt.
Higher freight coincided with deteriorating refining economics for US crude in Asia. WTI MEH cracking margins in Singapore averaged $14.72/b in October, falling about 37% from $23.31/b in September and roughly halving from $29.13/b in August.

Margins were stronger in other refining centers. WTI MEH cracking margins averaged $36.07/b in Northwest Europe and $46.94/b on the US Gulf Coast in October. Northwest European margins nevertheless fell from $47.47/b in September, while USGC margins slipped from $50.13/b.
A pullback in Asia-bound vessel fixtures suggests export demand could wane going forward. USGC crude fixtures to China fell to about 301,000 mt in October from 953,000 mt in September, while Far East fixtures dropped to 1.32 million mt from 4.30 million mt.
The pullback in fixtures offers an early indicator of pressure on future physical flows, even as October's realized export volumes held near 4.2 million b/d.
Shipping capacity tightens The freight surge comes against a tight global shipping
market, with disruptions around the Strait of Hormuz lengthening voyages and keeping ships at sea for longer.
Jan Kalfus, head of Americas chemical advisory at S&P Global Energy CERA, said that physical flows have held up better than expected despite the Hormuz disruption.
"We were maybe a little bit more pessimistic about the impact of the [Hormuz] closure," Kalfus said at the Houston International Maritime Conference Nov. 4, noting the strait "never really closed completely."
Supply has remained available, but at a cost. "We can still get the molecules, but... we pay premiums," Kalfus said, adding that inventories are low around the world. "Are we really ready for the next disruption because inventories are low all around the world?"
Kalfus said the shift has favored U.S. exporters. "This is a good time for the Americas," he said, calling the region "probably the winner of 2026."
WTI Midland averaged a 48 cents/b premium to Cushing WTI during October, down from 98 cents/b in September. Midland was 40 cents/b below WTI MEH, compared with an 82 cents/b discount in September.
The spread between Midland and WTI FOB USGC moved in the other direction. Midland averaged $2.28/b below WTI FOB USGC in October, compared with $1.83/b below it in September and $1.33/b below it in August. The widening coastal premium indicated stronger relative pricing for export barrels despite deteriorating freight economics.
-- Binish Azhar
Venezuela October crude exports fell 11%; heavy oil discounts widened
Shipments to US drop 35% month over month
India increases share to 27% of total exports
Heavy crude discounts widen $5/b in October
Venezuela's crude oil exports fell in October despite resilient production, as shipments to the US declined and widening discounts for Latin American heavy crude added pressure to the

country's oil industry. Venezuelan crude and condensate loadings totaled 28.4
million barrels in October, down from 32.2 million barrels in September and 29.9 million barrels in August, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea data. Shipments destined for the US plunged 35% to 11.5 million barrels from 17.6 million barrels in September, accounting for the largest decline among Venezuela's major export destinations.
The US remained Venezuela's largest crude export destination in October, but its share of total loadings declined to approximately 40% from 55% in September, according to CAS data.
The decline was partly offset by increased shipments to India, which received 7.7 million barrels in October, up from 6.8 million barrels in September. India's share of Venezuelan loadings increased to approximately 27% from 21%.
The weaker export performance contrasted with relatively stable upstream operations.
Venezuelan crude production held at about 1.21 million b/d in September, broadly steady from August, but around 125,000 b/d higher than a year earlier, with the gains increasingly concentrated in joint ventures, according to preliminary Ministry of Hydrocarbons data seen by Platts, part of S&P Global Energy.
PDVSA's JV operations in the Faja remained the largest contributor at 515,200 b/d, essentially flat month on month but up from 476,000 b/d in September 2025 and 426,000 b/d two years earlier. Western JVs produced 288,600 b/d, also little changed from August but about 36,000 b/d above September 2025 and more than 110,000 b/d above September 2024.
Regional heavy crude discounts widen The decline in Venezuelan exports coincided with a sharp
deterioration in pricing for several Latin American crude grades, reflecting pressure on heavy oil values relative to WTI.
Platts assessments showed the discount for Venezuelan Merey 16 widening by approximately $5/b between September and October, reaching $14.36/b below WTI.
Platts last assessed Venezuela's Merey 16 and Boscan crudes on a delivered-at-place basis to the US Gulf Coast at discounts of $28.50/b and $31.50/b, respectively, to the Latin American Brent Futures strip on Nov. 3.
The deterioration was even more pronounced for some competing regional grades.
Colombian Castilla's discount widened to approximately $17/b in October from $10/b in September, while Ecuadorian Napo weakened to around $22/b below WTI from $16/b. Colombian Vasconia, which had traded at a premium to WTI during parts of the second quarter, moved to a discount of $5.4/b in October from around $2/b in September.
Platts last assessed Colombia's Vasconia and Castilla crudes at discounts of $13.95/b and $29.45/b, respectively, to the Latin American Brent Futures strip on Nov. 3.
The weakness reflects increasingly competitive conditions in the US Gulf Coast refining market, where rising Canadian and

Pembina touts Pacific Link oil pipeline stake as growth driver
10% stake secured in Pacific Link project Q3 earnings rise 9% on NGL margin gains Asian propane exports boost revenue growth
Pembina Pipeline Corp. is touting its involvement in the 1 million-barrel/day Pacific Link crude oil pipeline to the Canadian

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

10

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

November 6, 2026

Pacific Coast as a driver of future growth, according to the company's third-quarter earnings call and report.
The Calgary-based midstream player's economic interest in the project will be 10% through construction of the project, with the opportunity to acquire an additional 10% interest when the project enters operation, the company's third-quarter interim report said.
Pembina is confident it will deliver its targeted growth through 2030, Scott Burrows, the company's president and CEO, said during a Nov. 6 earnings call. With the Pacific Link project and future expansions of the newly sanctioned Greenlight gas-fired power plant, the company is well positioned to extend that growth into the next decade, he said.
The Pacific Link pipeline, formerly known as the West Coast pipeline, will originate in Bruderheim, Alberta, and terminate at the Roberts Bank terminal in the southwest coast of British Columbia, according to the Alberta government.
The pipeline is a private-public partnership with other participants including the Alberta Petroleum Marketing Commission (representing the provincial government) and Trans Mountain Corp. (representing the federal government).
The project received a major boost on Oct. 1 when the federal government listed the proposed infrastructure as a "project of national importance," which kickstarted the environmental and regulatory processes for the project.
Higher NGL margins Pembina's underlying operating earnings grew 9% in the third
quarter compared to the third quarter of 2025, due to higher NGL margins, strong operational performance and higher volumes across the company's pipelines and facilities divisions, CFO Cameron Goldade said.
The higher NGL margins were "driven by the benefits from exposure to premium propane prices in Asian markets to West Coast exports and higher North American NGL prices," Goldade said.
When asked whether the exposure to strong Asian propane pricing is sustainable, Goldade said the company recognizes that the markets will ebb and flow. But Pembina has access to a range of markets, he said.
Pembina can ship propane to Asian markets through its export terminal at Prince Rupert and capacity at another facility; its pipeline network provides access to markets including the US Midwest and, through downstream connections, Eastern Canada; and its rail platform can move NGLs to markets throughout North America, Goldade said.
"We've seen the Asian market as being a very long-term and sustainable demand source. Which is why we've prioritized having that market access," Goldade said. "But finally, we do continue to think that having access to multiple markets is very valuable."
-- Kate Winston

Enbridge approves Canadian oil sands pipeline expansion
To serve CNOOC's Long Lake oil facility
Closes open season for Flanagan South
To acquire Laramie for US$485 million
Enbridge Inc. has sanctioned an expansion of the Long Lake oil sands pipeline in Alberta's Athabasca oil sands region to support CNOOC Petroleum North America's growing operations in the province, CEO Greg Ebel said Nov. 6.
Expansion of the oil sands pipeline will focus on adding a new lateral pipeline and storage tank, as well as expanding multiple lines in Enbridge's regional oil sands system like the Waupisoo and Wood Buffalo Extension pipelines, Ebel said on the company's third-quarter 2026 earnings webcast.
The project is expected to cost C$200 million ($142 million) and enter service in 2028, he said, without stating the capacity of the expanded pipeline.
Long Lake is CNOOC's Canadian oil sands facility that began production in 2008 with a capacity of 72,000 barrels/day, information on the company's website said.
In 2018, an expansion was initiated with an additional capacity of 26,000 b/d, CNOOC said, noting that bitumen produced from the steam-assisted gravity drainage facility is transported by pipeline to the Cheecham Terminal, where it connects to the Athabasca Pipeline System that is operated by Enbridge.
Approval of the Long Lake expansion intra-Western Canadian Sedimentary Basin is indicative of other such projects to follow, with Enbridge already in talks with "bigger" customers that are positioning for growth, even as smaller customers like CNOOC are moving ahead, he said.
The company is on the cusp of a huge cyclical and sustaining growth wave in Alberta's oil sands play and finds itself in a strong position with its seven trunk lines and multiple laterals, Enbridge said.
"In the spring of 2024, we expressed a strong view that supply growth out of Western Canada would reach 1 million b/d by 2035 and not many shared our view at that time. But today, that prediction is probably too low. This growth will require expansions of the Alberta regional oil sands business, increased diluent supply support for carbon sequestration and transportation, and increased capacity out of the basin," Ebel said.
Growing demand for WCSB crude In response to customer demand to move incremental
barrels from the WCSB to the US Midwest and the US Gulf Coast, Enbridge has successfully concluded an open season on the Flanagan South pipeline, he said.
"This is the third kind of expansion of Flanagan South, and we have another open season still open on Southern Access Extension, which will close later this year, with both trending to a FID of capital investments in those downstream pipes [by early

Gasoline
Brazil gasoline imports expected to reach 330,295 cubic meters in November
Netherlands supplies 35% of Brazil's fuel
Import arbitrage window closed for 254 days
Brazil is expected to receive at least 330,295 cubic meters of gasoline imports during November, according to preliminary data from port lineups, sources and S&P Global Commodities at Sea on Nov. 6.
Despite total gasoline imports of 330,295 cubic meters in the period, 32,000 cubic meters were attributed to state-owned Petrobras. This means that private-sector imports totaled approximately 298,295 cubic meters, excluding Petrobras volumes.
At least nine ships are scheduled and confirmed to discharge imported gasoline at Brazilian ports by November. Most will already have arrived, berthed, or discharged.

Gas pipeline open seasons Enbridge is making "good progress" on the proposed Project
Beacon expansion of the Algonquin Gas Transmission system, after completing an open season in July, President of Gas Transmission Matthew Akman said on the same webcast.
"We do see a strong potential for commercialization of this by very early in the new year," Akman said.
Project Beacon could add about 310,000 Dth/d of firm transport capacity into New England.
Enbridge is also seeking to commercialize two proposed Texas pipelines following recent open seasons. It held a nonbinding open season in September for the 2 billion cubic feet/day West Texas Project, which runs west from the Permian Basin to El Paso, Texas. And it launched, on Oct. 13, a nonbinding open season on the 2 Bcf/d Katy to Gillis Expansion Project, which would add capacity on the Texas Eastern Transmission system from East Texas into Louisiana.
"It's a little bit early, but we have seen strong indications of interest in both directions," Akman said.
The open seasons marked a shift from its recent Texas pipeline strategy, in which it has joined WhiteWater Midstreamled joint ventures to develop projects such as Blackcomb, Bay Runner and Eiger Express.
"It's more on our own in those areas because we can utilize some of our brownfield corridors," Akman said.
-- Ashok Dutta, Killian Staines

The Netherlands is set to be the top origin, accounting for 114,000 cubic meters, or 35% of November imports, underscoring the prevalence of products from the Amsterdam-RotterdamAntwerp hub. The US is the second-largest importer, with 103,295 cubic meters (31%), followed by Russia with 81,000 cubic meters (25%) and Spain with 32,000 cubic meters (10%).
Despite Russia's ban on fuel exports to secure domestic needs, two cargoes of Russia-origin gasoline appear as arriving in Brazil. Those are exported from Russia as naphtha and are blended in transit to meet Brazilian A-grade gasoline specifications, which have low octane numbers due to the mandatory final 32% anhydrous ethanol blend in the country.
For these international gasoline imports, the top two discharge ports are Suape (134,979 cubic meters) and Santos (61,000 cubic meters).
Russia has been reported as the most competitive origin for delivery laycans until early December, ranging from discounts of 45 cents to 30 cents against NYMEX December RBOB futures. By mid-December, the cheapest barrel shifts to ARA origin on the physical curve, with the discount narrowing to 25 cents.

SINGAPORE DATA: Light distillate stocks fall 2% amid higher gasoline exports
Gasoline exports rise 52.90% week over week
Naphtha imports from Southeast Asia rise 75.46%
Singapore's commercial stockpiles of light distillates fell 1.96% in the week of Oct. 29-Nov. 4 amid higher gasoline exports, despite higher naphtha imports, according to Enterprise Singapore data Nov. 5.
Stocks of light distillates -- which include gasoline, reformates and naphtha, but exclude gases such as LPG -- totaled 11.083 million barrels in the week to Nov. 4, the data showed.
The city-state's gasoline exports rose 52.90% to 742,836 metric tons in the week to Nov. 4, while gasoline imports fell 5.47% to 316,417 mt, according to the data.
The surge in gasoline exports came amid higher outflows to Indonesia, the region's largest buyer of low-octane gasoline, which increased 70.88% week over week to 332,371 mt, the data showed.
Meanwhile, the fall in gasoline imports came despite a 35.55% week-over-week rise in arrivals from China at 104.380 mt.
Chinese gasoline exports in November are expected to rise from the original estimate of about 300,000 mt to 800,000-1 million mt, following the release of new export quota volumes.
Net gasoline exports more than doubled to 426,419 mt from 151,079 mt the week before.

Naphtha imports rebound Singapore's imports of naphtha, reformates and other
blendstocks rebounded 75.46% week over week to 185,156 mt in the week to Nov. 4, the data showed.
The bulk of the imports came from the UAE at 35,312 mt, which rose by more than threefold from the previous week, and Trinidad and Tobago at 35,285 mt. No imports from Trinidad and Tobago were registered in the previous week.
Shipments from Russia totaled 31,700 mt, while inflows from Greece were at 30,128 mt. According to the data, there were no imports from either country the week before.
The city-state's exports of naphtha, reformates and other blendstocks surged by more than sevenfold to 35,246 mt in the week to Nov. 4. Exports headed to Malaysia were at 23,313 mt and Japan at 4,495 mt.
-- Joshua Ong, Norazlina Jumaat
Jet
NWE jet swap Mo1/Mo2 time spread widens as activity tips into 2027
Q1 2027 trading volume rises on arbitrage, airline hedging
Singapore-London spread neared $200/mt last week
The front-month/second-month Northwest European jet swap time spread rose to its highest level since August in the week beginning Nov. 2 as buying activity shifted toward the first quarter of 2027.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the spread at a weekly peak of a $22.75/mt contango Nov. 4, before it settled lower at $19.75/mt Nov. 5. The balance-of-month/Mo1 spread was also assessed wide at $18/mt Nov. 5
Exchange data pointed to increased trading activity in Q1 2027, with ICE jet CIF NWE cargoes versus frontline low sulfur gasoil futures showing January volumes of 113 lots, February at 148 lots and March at 83 lots. December recorded 141 lots, down 35.5% on the day, while February total volume was up almost threefold on the day.
Interest was shifting amid wide Singapore/London spreads, an uptick in airline hedging activity and a strong backwardation in the underlying ICE LSGO futures contract, traders said.
"Buying in Jan and Q1 is most popular and is driven by positioning and potential arbitrage," one Europe-based swaps trader said, adding that a lot of buying activity had come from Asia-based traders.
"It's quite an active Singapore market," the trader said. "The jet spread is incredibly wide. People are locking in that spread now."
Platts assessed the Mo2 jet CIF NWE swaps price at a $151.72/ mt average premium to the FOB Singapore equivalent in the week to Nov. 4, with highs of an almost $200/mt spread at the end of October.

Europe jet fuel, kerosene imports reach 1.2 million mt so far in November: CAS
Jet fuel, kerosene imports reach 1.2 million mt in Nov
UK European jet fuel intake at 145,000 mt in Nov
Europe's jet fuel and kerosene imports have reached roughly 1.2 million metric tons Nov. 1-6, more than half the total of around 2 million mt imported during all of October, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea data.
European jet fuel/kerosene imports in November so far include 213,000 mt from Nigeria, slightly lower compared with 301,000 mt imported in October; 373,000 mt from the UAE, much higher compared with 183,000 mt from last month; and 348,000 mt from South Korea, lower than the 415,000 mt imported in October.
The UK imported 145,000 mt of jet fuel Nov. 1-6, compared with the 733,000 mt total in October. The Netherlands has taken in 482,000 mt, much higher compared with last month's total of 142,000 mt. Italy has taken in 235,000 mt so far, compared with 200,000 mt in October, and Spain has taken in 144,000 mt in November so far, lower from 354,000 mt seen last month.
November jet fuel price differentials began on a weaker footing amid expectations of easing geopolitical tensions in the Middle East and continued diplomatic engagement between Washington and Tehran.
Platts assessed the Jet CIF NWE cargo differential at a $68.75/mt premium to ICE LSGO M1 Nov. 5, down $41.25/mt from Nov. 2.
"The market is better supplied in November than in October -- 100%," a trader source said. "We had some incremental volumes coming from different regions."
Observing jet fuel/kerosene imports into Europe so far this year, 4.3 million mt has been discharged from the US, 2.5 million mt from Nigeria, 1.6 million from India, 1.5 million mt from Kuwait, 1.5 million mt from Saudi Arabia and 1.5 million mt from UAE.
Looking ahead, according to Kpler, around 1.3 million mt of jet fuel is so far scheduled to arrive in Europe in November, so far lower than the 1.7 million mt that was imported in November 2025.

the week ended Nov. 1, well above the 105,000 mt imported the previous week, according to CAS data.
Of the imports during the week, 108,000 mt loaded from the US, 107,000 mt loaded from South Korea and 107,000 mt loaded from Nigeria.

Jet fuel stocks at the Amsterdam-Rotterdam-Antwerp refining hub rebounded from historically low levels, rising nearly 29% week over week to 490,000 metric tons, according to Nov. 5 data from Insights Global.
Inventories remain 55% lower than the volumes seen in the same week of 2025.
ARA jet fuel and kerosene stocks had fallen to 380,000 mt the previous week, the lowest level since September 2014, according to Insights Global data.
November jet fuel price differentials began on a weaker footing as market sentiment improved amid expectations of easing geopolitical tensions in the Middle East and continued diplomatic engagement between Washington and Tehran.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the Jet CIF NWE cargo differential at a $68.75/mt premium to ICE LSGO M1 on Nov. 5, down $41.25/mt from Nov. 2.

Of the October arrivals, 618,300 mt came from the Far East, 435,300 mt from the Middle East Gulf, 366,500 mt from the US, 334,900 mt from West Africa and 311,000 mt from India. Indian volumes were the highest since December 2025.
European jet market participants said that while some buyers remained hesitant to accept Indian-origin barrels because of EU sanctions concerns, the increase in flows reflected an acutely tight supply picture and stronger East-West arbitrage incentives.

Diesel
Brazilian diesel imports to reach 1.44 million cubic meters in November
Brazilian ULSD imports expected to stay high in November
US supplies 77% of Brazil's diesel volumes
Brazil is expected to maintain strong diesel imports in November, with volumes that should reach 1.44 million cubic meters, according to initial estimates based on S&P Global Commodities at Sea data, market participant input and port lineups. The expectation is broadly in line with the 1.48 million cu m anticipated in October, signaling continued strong demand for imported supply.
Importers remain active in the international market, supported by a government subsidy providing a 1,120 reais/cubic meter discount on imported ULSD. The mechanism is intended to encourage additional imports beyond Petrobras supply and ensure adequate domestic availability. However, with the provisional measure underpinning the subsidy set to expire in November, importers are accelerating purchases to secure the discount while it remains available, adding near-term support to import volumes amid uncertainty over whether the program will be extended.
Market participants also point to factors that could keep diesel imports elevated during a period when demand typically slows. One is Brazil's new fuel tax regime, expected to take effect next year. Sources expect cargoes imported before the change to clear customs under the current tax structure, potentially providing a pricing advantage and encouraging purchases ahead of the transition. Another factor is delayed grain planting progress following heavy rainfall across key producing regions, which could extend diesel demand from farm machinery and logistics beyond the usual seasonal window.
The new week's estimates showed that at least 31 tankers are scheduled to discharge at Brazilian ports during November, up by 2 tankers from the previous month's estimates.
Initial estimates show that Brazil is set to import about 1.05 million cubic meters of diesel in the first half of November, accounting for roughly 73% of the month's projected volume. Imports are expected to slow in the second half, with about

391,000 cubic meters, or 27% of the monthly total, scheduled to arrive at Brazilian ports between Nov. 16-30.
Supported by the subsidy, private-sector importers remain active and are expected to account for about 62% of Brazil's projected diesel imports in November. Petrobras, which is not eligible for the program, has about eight tankers scheduled to discharge during the month, representing the remaining 38% of expected volumes.
Russian supply is also returning to Brazil, with about 80,000 cubic meters scheduled to arrive aboard two tankers in November as Russian-origin offers reemerge in the international market. While Russia's official restrictions on ULSD exports remain in place, market participants have reported that some volumes continue to reach the export market under a quota system.
The US remains Brazil's largest diesel supplier, accounting for about 1.18 million cubic meters, or 77% of projected monthly imports. India ranks second at 245,000 cubic meters, or 17%, followed by Russia at 80,000 cubic meters, representing 6% of the total.
The Brazilian diesel import window has been closed for 297 days, according to Nov. 6 report from the Brazilian Association of Fuel Importers.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed US Gulf Coast DAP Santos cargoes at $4.3767/gal on Nov. 6, at a premium of 4.50 cents to NYMEX November ULSD futures, and all origins at $4.2142/gal on Nov. 6, at a discount of 12.25 cents to NYMEX November ULSD futures, for Medium Range tankers.
-- Renan Araujo

European diesel/gasoil imports expected to reach 2.4 million mt in Nov: Kpler
India is expected to ship about 1.2 million mt
About 599,000 mt of diesel/gasoil arrived on week
Europe's diesel/gasoil imports are expected to reach 2.4 million metric tons in November, down from 3.5 million mt in

October and below the 3.6 million mt imported in November 2025, according to Kpler shipping data.
Of this total, about 692,000 mt is expected to come from the US, lower than the 1 million mt that arrived from the US in October, but higher than the 547,000 mt that arrived in November 2025, according to Kpler. All is expected to come from PADD 3 (US Gulf Coast), with none expected to come from PADD 1 (Atlantic Coast).
Reduced monthly flows from the US come amid weaker transatlantic arbitrage incentives. The USGC- Europe arbitrage stood closed on Nov. 5 at minus $2.94/b, the lowest value since Oct. 6, Platts data showed.
The closed transatlantic arbitrage is expected to have a muted effect on fundamentals in Europe, according to traders, as record volumes from the East compensate for the lack of barrels from the US.
"US exports have been lower, certainly to Europe," a European diesel market participant said, adding that "there are strong flows from India [to Europe] and South Korea has capacity to export."
About 1.2 million mt of diesel/gasoil is expected to come from India in November, up from 1.1 million mt in September.
This could bring some relief, predominantly to the north, as "most cargoes go to NWE from India," said a European diesel trader.
A further 508,000 mt is expected to come from Saudi Arabia in November, up from 243,000 mt in October, according to Kpler.
Meanwhile, about 599,000 mt of diesel/gasoil was discharged in Europe in the week ended Nov. 1, the same amount as the previous week, according to Kpler. About 721,000 mt is expected to arrive in the week ending Nov. 8.

SINGAPORE DATA: Middle distillate stocks at 2-month low of 8.03 million barrels
Gasoil imports at 43,776 mt, supplied by India, Saudi Arabia
Jet fuel exports fall 60.84% week over week

Singapore's onshore commercial middle distillate stocks fell 5.55% week over week to 8.03 million barrels over Oct. 29-Nov. 4 as imports of both gasoil and jet fuel tumbled, data from Enterprise Singapore showed Nov. 5.
Stocks were last lower at 7.91 million barrels in the week ended Sept. 2, marking a two-month low, historical data showed.
The city-state remained a net exporter of gasoil over Oct. 29Nov. 4, with both inflows and outflows halving week over week.
Gasoil imports fell 48.49% week over week to 43,776 mt over Oct. 29-Nov. 4 amid a lack of inflows from North and Southeast Asia, the data showed.
Swing barrels from India at 33,757 mt and Saudia Arabia 10,018 mt accounted for the total imports over the period. Arrivals from Saudi Arabia were last seen over May 14-20 when Singapore received 20,218 mt from the Middle Eastern country, according to historical Enterprise Singapore data.
Meanwhile, gasoil exports tumbled to 241,518 mt in the

week to Nov. 4, from a near five-month high of 529,282 mt the previous week.
Outflows to Australia and Malaysia, the top two recipients in the previous week, fell by 76.29% and 51.10%, respectively, to 27,941 mt and 51,772 mt, the data showed. Exports to Papua New Guinea also fell 83.23% week over week to 4,720 mt.
Conversely, exports to Indonesia and Myanmar rose during the period to 46,167 mt and 34,790 mt, respectively, compared to 35,835 mt and 5,819 mt the week prior.
The remaining export volumes flowed to New Zealand at 45,511 mt, the Philippines at 26,648 mt and American Samoa at 3,923 mt, the data showed.
Jet fuel imports, exports fall Singapore remained a net exporter of jet fuel/kerosene over
Oct. 29-Nov. 4 as imports fell to 7.58 mt from 10,288 mt the week prior, according to Enterprise Singapore data.
Tracking the decline, exports fell 60.84% to 35,100 mt over the same period.
New Zealand was the largest destination with 24,465 mt, followed by Fiji with 8,019 mt and New Caledonia with 2,120 mt, the data showed.
-- Shu ling Lee, Mei huey Ng

BUNKERWORLD: Marine fuel prices fall to two-month low on weak crude, mixed demand
VLSFO, HSFO indices correct sharply
Singapore inventories rise amid export decline
Australia completes first green ammonia bunkering
Global marine fuel prices have fallen to their lowest in about two months, pressured by weaker crude futures and a mixed demand picture across the main trading hubs.
The Platts Bunkerworld Marine Fuel 0.5% Sulfur Index ended the week to Nov. 5 at $843/metric ton, the lowest since Sept. 10 and down $20/mt day over day, $91/mt week over week and $20/ mt month over month.
The Platts BW380 Index, which represents value for 3.5% sulfur fuel oil, ended the week at $674/mt, the lowest since Sept. 23. This was down $37/mt day over day, $131/mt week over week and $57/mt month over month.
The Hi-5 spread, the difference between 0.5%S and 3.5%S fuel oil, widened to $169.43/mt Nov. 5, its highest since Sept. 23.
Crude futures weakened significantly following the Democratic Party's sweeping US midterm election victory, which raised expectations of accelerated efforts to end the war in the Middle East.
Democratic US Senate minority leader Chuck Schumer and House Democratic minority leader Hakeem Jeffries have called on the Trump administration to end the Middle East war.

Las Palmas delivered bunker Hi5 spread hits multiyear high amid tight VLSFO
Las Palmas Hi-5 spread hits 45-month peak
VLSFO supply tightens across Mediterranean
Cargo premiums rise $60-$70/mt over barges
The Las Palmas delivered bunker Hi-5 spread -- the spread between very low sulfur and high sulfur fuel oil -- reached a 45-month high on Nov. 5.
This was due to strengthening Las Palmas-delivered VLSFO bunkers, which declined by $10/metric ton to $830/mt on Nov. 5, while the HSFO equivalent fell $20/mt to $680/mt, according to assessments by Platts, part of S&P Global Energy.

The Hi5 spread was therefore assessed at $150/mt, with VLSFO falling more slowly than HSFO after two consecutive days of strength.
Market participants attributed the recent strength to a tighter fuel supply picture at the port due to delayed cargoes, with prompt VLSFO facing a greater supply challenge than HSFO.
Several suppliers were unable to offer prompt product, with one reporting limited availability for much of November, according to a source.
Due to high VLSFO prices in Las Palmas, lifting fuel in Lisbon has become more economical, the source said.
"We're tight on VLSFO in both Las Palmas and Gibraltar. We have product, but we can only offer quite high," a supplier said. "There are tight cargo avails throughout the Med."
In the bulk VLSFO market, market participants noted an increase in cargo premiums for product across the basin, driven by tighter supply and increased volumes leaving the region to Singapore.
"It's [VLSFO] all going to Singapore for the last month," a European trader said, supported by a wider East/West spread.
The paper 0.5%S Rotterdam barge structure also reflected this tightness, with the balance-month/front-month timespread assessed at $26.25/mt on Nov. 5, close to a four-month high.
The crack equivalent also strengthened, flipping from negative to positive on Nov. 5 at $2.679/b.
In the Mediterranean, market participants said premiums for delivered cargoes had risen into the $60-$70/mt range over 0.5% Rotterdam barges.
-- Alexander Wong, Tommy Petrou, Iman Rezig

VLSFO supply tightens on delays, component shortages
HSFO remains better supplied despite constraints
European fuel oil fundamentals diverged as tight very low sulfur fuel oil supply contrasted with greater high sulfur fuel oil availability across the region in the week to Nov. 6.
The European VLSFO complex has grown tighter across Northwest European and Mediterranean hubs amid component bottlenecks, loading delays and arbitrage movement out of the region.
Several market participants have noted "loading delays" at terminals within the Amsterdam-Rotterdam-Antwerp region, which have tightened availability in Northwest Europe on the week.
Compounding this is a tighter market for blending components, which limits the availability of on-spec RMGgrade VLSFO.
A European trader said, "It's tighter in ARA as it's more expensive to blend ... lack of shale and components".
Within the Mediterranean, a wider East/West spread has also supported stable flows out of the region, limiting the availability

blending constraints limited availability of finished RMG-grade material.

"There's oil on offer for sure, issue is RMG finished grade," a trader said.

Singapore also imported 78,241 mt of fuel oil from Europe in the week to Nov. 4, of which 39,854 mt came from France and the remainder from Italy. However, there were no fuel oil arrivals from Russia in the latest week, the data showed.
Fuel oil imports from the Middle East fell nearly 9% from the previous week to 341,331 mt, of which 145,559 mt came from the UAE, about 109,049 mt from Iraq, 50,501 mt from Kuwait, and the rest from Oman, the data showed.
Singapore's inventory data counts only stocks at onshore terminals. Enterprise Singapore describes heavy distillates as "residues," which include cracked and straight run fuel oil and low sulfur waxy residue.
In the downstream market, Singapore bunker premiums for the low and high fuel oil grades fell amid more aggressive selling, on the back of a weaker fuel oil complex in the week to Nov. 5, according to local traders.
While spot inquiry flows were modest for most of the week, persistent constraints on prompt availability limited trades for H1 November deliveries, traders noted.

Feedstocks
ARA naphtha stocks fall 4.8% in week to Nov. 5, gasoline up 2.9%: Insights Global
Singapore receives record 2.7 mil mt in October: CAS
Gasoline stocks climb 2.88% despite weak demand
Naphtha stocks in the Amsterdam-Rotterdam-Antwerp hub declined 4.81% in the week ended Nov. 5 to 257,000 metric tons, Insights Global data showed.
Inventories fell 51.51% year over year, indicating substantially tighter supply conditions from the previous year.
While demand has slowed down in the past week, supply availability remains extremely thin.
"It feels quiet -- both thin supply and thin demand across all grades," a trading source said.
Strong arbitrage economics for eastbound flows from the Atlantic basin supported the movement of barrels to hubs such as Singapore.
Singapore emerged as a major outlet for Mediterranean and North African naphtha in October, with flows rising to a record high of 2.7 million mt from just 85,100 mt in September, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea data.
The jump made Singapore the second-largest destination for Mediterranean and North African naphtha in October, narrowly behind the Netherlands, which received 2.8 million mt.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the physical naphtha CIF NWE cargo price at $851.25/mt on Nov. 5.
Platts assessed the front-month CIF NWE naphtha swap down $17.50/mt on Nov. 5 to $798.00/mt, while the crack was up 35 cents/barrel at $4.00/b.
Platts assessed the November/December time spread at $35.00/mt on the same day, down $1.00/mt day over day, while the December/January spread was up $2.50/mt at $23.50/mt.
Gasoline inventories rise 2.88% ARA gasoline inventories rose 2.88% to 893,000 mt in the
week ended Nov. 5, reflecting a weekly increase of 25,000 mt, according to the latest Insights Global data. However, stocks were 15.6% lower year over year.

Despite the weekly inventory build, gasoline fundamentals remained relatively firm, with the modest stock increase suggesting steady demand absorption in the regional market.

Asian polyethylene-naphtha spread widens amid cautious downstream markets
Naphtha drops $154/mt; PE markets mixed
HDPE-naphtha spread widens to $261.75/mt
Converters resist stockpiling despite margins
Asian polyethylene prices diverged against key upstream naphtha in the week to Nov. 6, however, the widened spread failed to spur PE converter demand amid a cautious market outlook.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the C+F Japan naphtha marker at $928.25/metric ton Nov. 6, down $154/ mt from its fourth quarter-to-date high of $1,082.25/mt Oct. 30. CFR Far East Asia high-density polyethylene film closed at $1,190/mt Nov. 6, its highest this quarter so far and $10/mt above Oct. 30. Linear low-density polyethylene C4 butene fell $25/mt to $1,180/mt, below its Nov. 2 quarterly high of $1,225/ mt. General-purpose low-density polyethylene dropped $40/mt to $1,360/mt, compared with its $1,410/mt peak Nov. 2-3, Platts data showed.
The HDPE-naphtha spread widened $164/mt week over week to $261.75/mt, LLDPE-naphtha widened $129/mt to $251.75/mt and LDPE-naphtha widened $114/mt to $431.75/mt. However, the wider spreads mainly reflected naphtha's sharper weekly decline, not uniformly stronger polymer prices.
Naphtha softened, tracking the upstream crude market, although supply tightness has supported cash differentials. Previously, high feedstock, production and freight costs enabled polymer sellers to defend offers, but converters in China and Southeast Asia bought on a need-to basis and resisted price increases to protect margins. LLDPE showed the clearest strength earlier in the week. Agricultural film demand supported HDPE despite weaker packaging orders, while slower domestic trade weighed on LDPE, participants said.
The wider spreads may ease pressure along the production chain but may not be evidence of increased end-user

demand due to buyers' reluctance to build inventories, market participants have said.
Participants expected demand to be cautious in the near term. Buyers are likely to continue purchasing only as needed, with little appetite for inventory building, especially as uncertainty persists over the sustainability of higher polymer prices.
-- Zhi Xuan Ho
Gas Liquids
Platts Asian LPG Daily Commentary
North Asia LPG prices surge $75/mt Nov. 6
Asian propane-naphtha swaps spread widens
CFR North Asia LPG prices rose at the close of Asian trading on Nov. 6 after four consecutive sessions of declines, driven by a $33.68/metric ton rise in front-month Brent futures, as geopolitical risks remained in focus and markets reassessed the situation.
According to an Asia-based trader, tight first-half December propane availability, coupled with a renewed rise in crude prices, could lend support to LPG prices.
A regional broker also noted limited propane volumes heading to the Far East, contributing to tighter first-half December supply. The broker attributed the reduced availability to winter-related domestic stockpiling of propane and butane, as well as loading delays and operational disruptions.
Platts assessed CFR North Asia propane and butane cargoes at $1,060.50/mt and $1,150.50/mt, respectively, on Nov. 6, both up $75/mt from the previous session.
Platts assessed CFR East China propane at $995/mt on Nov. 6, up $61/mt from the previous session.
Platts assessed CFR South China propane at $991/mt on Nov. 6, up $61/mt day over day.
Two bids were heard during the Nov. 6 trading session, but no trades were concluded at the market close.
Platts assessed the CFR North Asia propane-to-C&F Japan naphtha front-month swap spread at $54/mt on Nov. 6, widening from $34.75/mt in the previous session.
In tender activity, Vietnam's PV Gas seeks term LPG for 2027 delivery to Vungtau, Vietnam on a DES basis, Platts reported earlier. The tender closes Nov. 12, with next-day validity.
In chemical news, Japan's Maruzen Petrochemical plans to shut down permanently its naphtha-fed steam cracker at Chiba from the second half of November, Platts reported earlier.
The cracker can produce 490,000 mt/year of ethylene and 278,000 mt/year of propylene, as well as an undisclosed amount of crude C4 -- the feedstock for butadiene production, the news added.

Tankers
Hormuz crossings rise despite continued ship attacks, high insurance premiums
Hormuz traffic rises to 26 vessels Nov. 5
IRGC claims two ships collided with mines
High insurance premiums persist
Ship traffic through the Strait of Hormuz ticked up to 26 transits Nov. 5 compared with 21 the day prior, according to the latest report from S&P Global Commodities at Sea, even as government and security sources continued to report ship attacks in the region.
On Nov. 6, the UK Maritime Trade Operations Centre reported an explosion on a tanker 16 nautical miles east of Khasab, Oman, when the ship exited Hormuz. The British government agency verified 11 incidents between Oct. 29 and Nov. 4, compared with three in the prior week.
Iran's Revolutionary Guard Corps claimed Nov. 6 that two oil tankers passing through what it calls an unauthorized route inside the strait hit mines and caught fire, according to a statement by state news agency IRNA.
A Marshall Islands-flagged products tanker, Velos Sapphire, was struck Nov. 4 and damaged by two projectiles off the coast of Madinat ash Shamal, Qatar, Vanguard Tech said separately in a Nov. 6 update.
CAS data showed the vessel had passed through the Strait of Hormuz Nov. 4 and was heading for Sitra, Bahrain. The International Maritime Organization said one of the crew members aboard -- a Filipino national -- was killed during the incident, marking the 25th seafarer casualty since the outbreak of the US-Iran war on Feb. 28.
The additional war risk premium for Hormuz passage has been as high as 12.5% of a ship's hull value in recent months, compared with 0.5% for loadings east of Hormuz, according to a Nov. 6 emailed note from brokerage Marsh's global head of marine, Marcus Baker.
AIS off As ship attacks have persisted and security remains uncertain
in the waterway, most vessels are opting to turn off their automatic identification systems, and 17 of the vessels that moved through the strait Nov. 5 had interrupted or limited visibility, CAS said.
Three crude tankers entered the contested waterway, while one exited the strait, with the seven-day average for crude exports loaded at ports inside the Gulf region totaling 3.68 million barrels/day, according to the CAS report.
The CAS data showed that 11 vessels that passed through the Strait of Hormuz were linked to Iran or sanctioned, including the Very Large Gas Carrier Searambler, which is sanctioned by the US Treasury Department.
"During its previous voyage, the vessel carried Iranian LPG before conducting a transfer operation near Malaysia, with the

cargo subsequently delivered to China," the report said. "The vessel has now reentered the Gulf and appears to be positioning for its next loading program."
Another sanctioned LPG carrier, the VLGC Marser, exited the Gulf laden. It "continues to play a key role in transporting Iranian LPG cargoes from the region," the report said.
Just beyond the Strait of Hormuz at the US blockade line, traffic rose to 76 vessels, the highest daily total recorded since blockade measures were reinstated July 14 and a record for the current enforcement period, excluding the June 18-July 13 suspension. Of those, only three were sanctioned or linked to Iran, CAS said.
Across the Arabian Peninsula at the Bab al-Mandab Strait, tanker activity eased to 13 movements from 16 the day before, CAS data showed.
-- Lauren Holtmeier, Max Lin, Aresu Eqbali
Odfjell sees spot shipping volumes rise as volatility weighs on contracts
Freight rates drop 30%-40% West of Suez
Terminal occupancy steady at 95%
Norwegian shipowner Odfjell saw spot shipping volumes rise in the third quarter as global market volatility weighed on contractual business, the company said during its earnings call Nov. 5.
Spot charter rates softened during the quarter, but were offset by higher shipping volumes. The company's volumes increased 3.6 million metric tons during the period, with the increase largely driven by spot market volumes.
"We saw a significant uptick when it came to transportation of commodity chemicals and the big increases were due to increased volumes of products like caustic soda, BTX, and methanol," said CEO Harald Fotland.
Contractual shipments remained stable, with the company seeing no contract renewals during the quarter.
"Only natural [to not see any new contract renewals], as our customers are prevented from taking long-term positions in today's very volatile market," Fotland said.
Freight rates fell during the quarter, with rates West of Suez dropping 30%-40% as vessels relocated from eastern trades to the Atlantic region, while East of Suez rates edged higher. Swing trade remained at historic lows, with only 2% of Medium Range tankers entering the chemical segment, according to Fotland.
During the Q&A, Fotland said that a peace treaty and the reopening of the Strait of Hormuz could lead to increased transportation volumes, driven by the need to restock inventories.
Odfjell sold two vessels during the quarter, Bow Elm and Bow Lind, and signed agreements for two additional super segregators from Japan's Kitanihon Shipbuilding.
Tank occupancy at the company's terminal remained steady at 95%.

Platts Americas Dirty Tanker Daily Commentary
VLCC USGC-China route falls $3 million further
Suezmax USGC-UKC run drops w5 after failed fixture
Aframax trans-Atlantic run holds steady at last w325
Bearish market fundamentals persisted in the Americas Dirty tanker segment on Nov. 6, with muted activity heading into the weekend pushing rates lower.
After the close of the Nov. 5 Market on Close assessment process, one shipbroker said that the arbitrage to Asia was shut, so the VLCC US Gulf Coast-China route should see freight at $50 million or lower. That sentiment largely continued on Nov. 6, as the bulk of market indications for the route were heard in the $47.5 million-$53 million range.
A second shipbroker who called the run at $50 million said that freight for the route "will be softer if it ever does get tested."
Platts assessed the benchmark 270,000 metric ton USGCChina run down $3 million to $50 million.
Indications also pushed lower for the VLCC Brazil-China route following reports that a ship had been booked at w500 for the related West Africa-China voyage.
"I think we see that [run] under significant pressure," a third broker said about the Brazil-China voyage. "Would not be surprised to see w450 done over the next couple of trading days."
Market indications were heard between w465-w500, with the bulk still focused toward the higher end of the range.
Freight for the 260,000 mt Brazil-China run finished the day w55 lower to w495.
For the Suezmaxes, lackluster activity and reports of failed fixtures saw market calls shift lower to end the week. After reports that the Prudent Warrior, which was booked on Nov. 5 for a USGC-Transatlantic run at w295, had failed subjects, calls largely shifted to a range of w285-w295.
Platts assessed the 145,000 mt USGC-UK Continent run down w5 to w290.
A fourth shipbroker said that charterers had been left with a "greater scope to test owners' appetite on routes that have yet to see fresh business."
Platts also assessed the benchmark 130,000 mt Guyana-UKC route w15 lower to w385 on softened market indications.

Two transatlantic fixtures were reported on Nov. 6, with Trafigura booking the Seascout loading Nov. 21-23 for a USGCUKC run at w325 with a $90,000/day demurrage deemed the latest deal. Two shipbrokers referred to w325 as a "floor" after the rate and demurrage were repeated by the Tenacity Venture to Mercuria, as reported by Platts on Nov. 5.
"Seems a nice floor for owners to secure while having [charterers] feel a little relief in rates," a fifth shipbroker said.
Platts assessed the benchmark 70,000 mt USGC-UKC route unchanged at w325. Multiple market participants have blamed prompt tonnage availability and charterers "holding out" cargoes for the precipitous rate drop seen during the week.
Rates on the West Coast continued to edge lower, with active market indications for long-haul and local runs reflecting bearish sentiment.
Platts assessed the benchmark 80,000 mt Vancouver-China and Vancouver-US West Coast routes at $9.75 million and w775, respectively. The assessed values are the lowest for each benchmark since Oct. 5 and 6, respectively.
"Definitely pressure there as well," a sixth shipbroker said of rates out of Vancouver.

News
China targets 35% electricity share in final energy use by 2030
Charging network to support over 110 mil EVs
2030 Renewable H2 capacity aimed at 2 mil mt/yr
Coal-to-oil, gas strategic bases to expand
China aims to increase electricity's share of final energy consumption to about 35% by 2030 and to expand renewable hydrogen production capacity to 2 million mt/year by the same year, as Beijing accelerates the substitution of oil and gas to reduce import risks and support its energy transition, the National Development and Reform Commission said at a Nov. 6 press briefing.
Oil and natural gas currently account for about one-third of China's final energy consumption, concentrated primarily in the transportation and industrial sectors, leaving significant scope for substitution with electricity, hydrogen and green fuels, NDRC official Ge Ju said.
The substitution push aims to reduce China's exposure to oil and gas imports, strengthen domestic energy security, and support the country's energy transition and carbon-peaking and carbon-neutrality goals, Ge said.

Aframax rates steady to softer Aframax rates in the Atlantic Basin held steady as fresh
testing suggested rates found support.

Electrification China will continue to expand electrification, particularly in
transportation.

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

23

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

November 6, 2026

New energy vehicles accounted for 53.6% of total vehicle sales in China during January-September 2026, Ge said.
By 2030, China aims to have more than 40 million electric vehicle charging facilities, providing charging capacity for more than 110 million EVs.
The government plans to develop charging infrastructure across urban areas, highways, and rural regions and to expand smart charging and vehicle-to-grid applications.
The expansion is expected to increase electricity's share of final energy use to about 35% by 2030, the NDRC said.
Hydrogen, green fuels China will also expand renewable hydrogen and green fuels
during 2026-30. The government plans to develop clusters of green hydrogen,
ammonia and methanol projects in Northeast China, the Yellow River bend region, northern North China and the northern foothills of the Tianshan Mountains.
China's renewable hydrogen production will support the use of green fuel across power generation, transportation, chemicals, metallurgy, and large-scale energy storage, according to the NDRC.
The government also plans to promote renewable-powered hydrogen production through direct green-power connections, off-grid renewable projects, and broader hydrogen demonstration applications.
Coal-to-oil, gas Despite the drive toward electrification and green fuels,
China will also retain coal-to-oil and coal-to-gas as part of its energy-security strategy, reflecting the country's abundant coal resources and relatively limited domestic oil and gas reserves.
During 2026-30, China plans to strengthen strategic capacity and technology reserves for coal-to-oil and coal-to-gas production and to advance strategic production bases in Ordos, Inner Mongolia; Yulin, Shaanxi; and Zhundong and Hami, Xinjiang, according to the NDRC.
The policy comes as new coal-to-gas capacity continues to come online. China Datang started production at the first phase of its Fuxin coal-to-gas project in Liaoning this month, with a capacity of 1.33 Bcm/year. The broader project is designed to reach an eventual capacity of 4 Bcm/year.
-- Cindy Liang - LNG Market Specialist
Ukraine hits fourth Russian refinery in November: reports
Drone strike ignites tank farm at refinery
Four facilities targeted since month began
2.1 million b/d capacity remains offline
Ukraine attacked the Afipsky refinery in Krasnodar Krai Nov. 6, according to Russian media, marking the most hostile start to a month for Russian refineries since August.

The tank farm at the 180,000 barrels/day Afipsky refinery caught fire following a drone attack and smoke was visible over the main refinery complex, according to an OSINT analyst at Russian media outlet Astra. Astra examined a photograph taken by an eyewitness in Krasnodar and later published a video of the fire.
"In the urban-type settlement of Afipsky in the Seversky District, a fire broke out on the premises of an enterprise following the impact of drone debris," the Krasnodar Krai operational headquarters said in a Telegram post Nov.6.
The strike on the Krasnodar Krai facility was the fourth attack on a Russian refinery since the start of November, marking the most intense period of attacks on Russian refineries since August.
The period follows a slowdown in attacks in October triggered by pressure from the US administration on Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy. President Donald Trump repeatedly blamed Ukrainian attacks on Russia for high diesel prices. Ukraine avoided attacking Russian refineries for two consecutive weeks from Oct. 16 to Oct. 29.
Ukraine will continue attacking Russian refineries until Moscow stops striking its energy infrastructure or until Washington meets a separate air defense request, Zelenskyy said Nov. 1.
About 2.1 million b/d of primary refining capacity was offline as of the end of October, according to S&P Global Energy CERA analysts. They estimated offline capacity at about 3.1 million b/d at the end of September.
-- Alexandra Vladimirova
